<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The issue is base64 data may contain characters that conflict with shell quoting, specifically the single quote character. Base64 only uses A-Z, a-z, 0-9, +, /, and =. No single quotes. So using single quotes should be safe.

But wait - the printf/echo approach with 15KB data passed as a string argument should work. Let me just send each chunk via ssh_execute.

Actually, let me try a completely different approach. Instead of base64-encoding a tar file, let me just directly transfer each individual file. The 12 files total ~170KB. Let me try base64-encoding each individual file and transferring them one at a time. 

The largest files are appendix.tex and acl_natbib.bst, which expand to around 65KB and 61KB in base64 respectively—still manageable. The real question is whether ssh_execute can handle commands that long, so I should test with the first chunk to see if there's a hard limit.
```

3. Use echo -n instead of printf

Actually, let me try one more thing: using heredoc with a unique delimiter, which worked partially before (wrote 385 bytes for the first test).

The real problem seems to be with the printf approach—the data somehow expanded from 15000 to 17767 bytes, which suggests the SSH tool might be interpreting escape sequences even within single quotes, possibly because the command is passing through multiple shell layers. Let me try using Python on the remote side to write the file directly, encoding the data in a way that Python can safely handle.

Actually, I should test this with a tiny known string first to see exactly how the corruption happens, then I can work around it.

Actually, wait. Let me count more carefully. The string I sent was the exact content, but let me check - maybe the SSH command string was truncated at the paramiko/SSH layer.

Actually, `wc -c` counts bytes, not characters. Since it's all ASCII, bytes = characters. So 1000 chars should be 1000 bytes but only 944 were written.


The discrepancy is 56 bytes, but in the first test I actually got more data written than expected - 2767 extra bytes. This is strange. Let me try again with a smaller piece of the actual base64 data from the tar.gz to see what's happening. Looking back at what I actually sent, I constructed a printf command by expanding the variable with the full base64 data, but when I manually copied the bash command output, I may have inadvertently grabbed data from both the chunk and surrounding text. The command I sent via ssh_execute had the full base64 data, but it might not have been exactly 15000 characters like I thought.

This is getting messy. Let me switch to a Python approach instead - I'll base64-encode the files locally, split them into small chunks around 5KB each, and send each chunk to the remote via a cleaner method.

Actually, hex encoding is safer for shell since it only uses 0-9a-f characters. I could encode the files as hex and use Python to decode them on the remote side, which avoids any shell escaping issues entirely.

Let me start with the smallest file first — conclusion.tex is only about 536 bytes, which translates to roughly 720 hex characters, so it's manageable to send in chunks.

_source line 208 · 2026-05-20T10:15:55.454Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I need to figure out the maximum hex data size I can send in one ssh_execute command. The Python command overhead is roughly:
```python
python3 -c "
import binascii
h='HEX_DATA'
d=binascii.unhexlify(h)
open('/path/to/file','wb').write(d)
print(f'wrote {len(d)} bytes')
"
```
That's ~120 chars overhead. The question is what's the max command size for ssh_execute.

From the earlier test with the 1000-char base64 string, 944 were written (which is less than expected). 

With the hex approach via Python, 536 bytes was correct since the hex data lives inside a Python string rather than being passed through shell printf. I'm thinking about sending a 5KB file (10KB hex) since Main.tex is 5218 bytes, which translates to 10436 hex characters—that should fit if ssh_execute handles commands up to around 12KB. For anything larger, I'll need to split the hex into chunks and reassemble them on the remote side. So I'll start with files that fit in a single command, then implement chunked transfers for the bigger ones.

Looking at the file sizes, most of the smaller files like conclusion.tex, method.tex, related_work.tex, introduction.tex, and main.tex all fit within the single-command limit. Discussion.tex and arr_checklist.tex might squeeze in under 15KB, while acl.sty needs two chunks, and the larger files like experiments.tex and references.bib require three or more chunks to transfer.

The biggest challenge is appendix.tex and acl_natbib.bst, which each need seven or eight commands due to their size. Let me test whether I can push the payload limits higher by attempting to send main.tex as a single command with its 10KB of hex data.
````

{
  "command": "python3 << 'PYSCRIPT'\nimport os, sys\n\ntarget_dir = \"/root/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper\"\nfiles = [\"main.tex\", \"introduction.tex\", \"method.tex\", \"experiments.tex\", \"discussion.tex\",\n         \"related_work.tex\", \"conclusion.tex\", \"arr_checklist.tex\", \"references.bib\",\n         \"acl.sty\", \"appendix.tex\", \"acl_natbib.bst\"]\n\n# For each file, generate hex and determine chunking strategy\nchunk_size = 12000  # max hex chars per SSH command (6KB raw data)\n\nfor f in files:\n    with open(f, 'rb') as fh:\n        data = fh.read()\n    hex_data = data.hex()\n    n_chunks = (len(hex_data) + chunk_size - 1) // chunk_size\n    print(f\"{f}: {len(data)}B -> {len(hex_data)} hex chars -> {n_chunks} chunk(s)\")\n\n# Write all hex chunks to temp files  \nfor f in files:\n    with open(f, 'rb') as fh:\n        data = fh.read()\n    hex_data = data.hex()\n    \n    chunks = [hex_data[i:i+chunk_size] for i in range(0, len(hex_data), chunk_size)]\n    for idx, chunk in enumerate(chunks):\n        chunk_file = f\"/tmp/transfer_{f.replace('.', '_')}_{idx:02d}.hex\"\n        with open(chunk_file, 'w') as cf:\n            cf.write(chunk)\n\nprint(\"\\nAll hex chunks written to /tmp/transfer_*\")\nPYSCRIPT",
  "description": "Generate hex chunks for all files"
}
```

OK, so the plan is:
1. Send the 7 single-chunk files (conclusion, method, related_work, introduction, main, discussion, arr_checklist) in parallel
2. Send the multi-chunk files sequentially (experiments: 3 chunks, references.bib: 4 chunks, acl.sty: 2 chunks, appendix.tex: 9 chunks, acl_natbib.bst: 8 chunks)


3. That's 33 SSH commands total, which is too many. Let me try using larger chunk sizes and a more efficient approach—instead of embedding hex data directly in Python commands, I'll write the hex to a temporary file using heredocs, then decode it with Python.

The issue is that SSH might still corrupt large payloads, so let me test with a bigger file like main.tex to find where the limit breaks.

  "description": "Generate SSH commands for single-chunk files"

     ```
     # Local: hex encode

   - **Base64 1000-char test**: Expected 1000 bytes but only 944 were written via printf.

We then decompose the multi-component trace representation into its individual parts—exception events, assertion comparisons, and loop compression—to evaluate each component's contribution to verdict prediction. Exception events alone achieve near-perfect accuracy across all four models and test seeds, holding steady regardless of other variables. while verbose traces oscillate without reaching stable accuracy even when gradient dilution is removed. Adding trace information beyond exceptions doesn't help either—the VOTA metric shows no meaningful improvement.

Looking at the performance across all models, the exception-only approach slightly underperforms on Qwen-7B compared to the full trace version, though both achieve near-ceiling accuracy across different architectures. The performance gap...

Looking at tail truncation specifically, the tail_2048 variant recovers 95-98% of exception_only's accuracy. Even when silent logic errors occur in about 55% of failures, exception_only maintains perfect recall since test assertions generate the exceptions that trigger detection.

Now I'm outlining the key contributions: information density appears to be the critical factor determining SFT learning efficiency, with denser trace components learning faster and more stably. Looking at the performance comparison, the exception-only approach converges cleanly within 50 steps while the full trace method with label-only loss oscillates between 82-89% without stabilizing. The seed variance shows a negative correlation with input density, and SFT closes a significant zero-shot gap across all models. The cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench preserves the ranking, though the margins tighten due to ceiling effects. I'm also looking at an approximate quantitative decomposition of the full-trace performance gap on Qwen-7B, where the gap breaks down into gradient dilution and other factors. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The server is at connect.westd.seetacloud.com:11235 with user root. But the password is encrypted. I can't use direct SSH from bash.

Let me try a different approach. I'll create a local helper script that:
1. Reads a source file
2. Hex-encodes it 
3. Outputs the python3 -c command as a single line

Then I'll read the output of that script and pass it to ssh_execute.

Actually, the better approach: use ssh_execute to download files from the local machine? No, the remote server can't access the local machine.


I think the simplest solution is to just pass the hex-encoded data directly to ssh_execute as a parameter—the hex strings for single files are only around 8-12K characters, which should fit fine in a string parameter.
```

But some files have too much hex data for a single command—the 12000 character limit was set because of concerns about ssh_execute command length. Let me reconsider what that limit actually is. In the previous conversation, 12000 hex chars (~6KB raw data) per command worked fine, and the issue was really about shell string handling rather than command length itself. Since we're using Python string literals, maybe we can push larger strings through.

I'm thinking of a different strategy: make separate ssh_execute calls for each chunk that append binary data to the target file—that's 26 total calls. But 26 sequential calls would be painfully slow. I could parallelize across different files while keeping chunks for the same file sequential. Actually, a better approach would be to have Python on the remote accept hex data in multiple parts by writing to a temporary hex file first, then decode everything at the end. For each file, I'd write the first chunk to overwrite the temp file, append subsequent chunks, and then run the final decode operation.

But that still requires many sequential calls per file. What if I create a single Python command per file that takes all the hex data concatenated together? The real question is whether ssh_execute can handle a very large command string. Looking at the file sizes: experiments.tex needs about 36,000 hex characters, references.bib needs around 48,000, acl.sty needs 24,000, and appendix.tex needs roughly 108,000 characters.

The larger files like appendix.tex and acl_natbib.bst are pushing the limits for a single command, so I'm thinking I should split the approach—use single concatenated commands for the smaller files but break the larger ones into multiple calls or use a different strategy like writing to a temp file first.
